    "oldString": "SwiGLU feed-forward networks~\\cite{shazeer2020glu} in modern LLMs exhibit high contextual sparsity: at any given token, a large fraction of FFN neurons contribute negligibly to the output.\nLearned sparsity predictors~\\cite{liu2023dejavu,gautam2026fastforward} exploit this property by training small networks to anticipate which neurons are important, but they universally rely on binary cross-entropy (BCE) loss, treating each neuron as an independent classification target.",
    "newString": "SwiGLU feed-forward networks~\\cite{shazeer2020glu} in modern LLMs exhibit high contextual sparsity: at any given token, a large fraction of FFN neurons contribute negligibly to the output.\nWhile our distillation objective is architecture-agnostic, SwiGLU's gated activation makes sparsity prediction particularly challenging: neuron importance depends on both gate and up-projection values, creating a non-trivial prediction target that magnitude-based methods handle suboptimally.\nLearned sparsity predictors~\\cite{liu2023dejavu,gautam2026fastforward} exploit this property by training small networks to anticipate which neurons are important, but they universally rely on binary cross-entropy (BCE) loss, treating each neuron as an independent classification target.",
    "originalFile": "\\section{Introduction}\n\nSwiGLU feed-forward networks~\\cite{shazeer2020glu} in modern LLMs exhibit high contextual sparsity: at any given token, a large fraction of FFN neurons contribute negligibly to the output.\nLearned sparsity predictors~\\cite{liu2023dejavu,gautam2026fastforward} exploit this property by training small networks to anticipate which neurons are important, but they universally rely on binary cross-entropy (BCE) loss, treating each neuron as an independent classification target.\nYet at inference, sparsity budgets are allocated globally: the TEAL (training-free activation sparsity;~\\citealp{liu2025teal}) top-$k$ mechanism ranks all neurons across all layers and retains only those above a model-wide threshold.\nThis mismatch between per-neuron training and global inference allocation has not been formally analyzed.\n\nGradient analysis reveals a structural asymmetry: BCE's gradient $\\partial \\mathcal{L}_{\\text{BCE}} / \\partial s_j = \\sigma(s_j) - m_j^*$ depends only on neuron $j$, while under KL divergence, the gradient couples each neuron to all others through the downstream Jacobian (\\S\\ref{sec:method:gradient}).\nKL's coupled gradients discover neuron importance patterns that diverge 68\\% from magnitude-based masks (per-layer Jaccard 32\\%).\nA target-swap ablation confirms that these discovered mask targets are the proximate quality driver at inference: BCE trained on KL-derived targets reproduces KL quality (max $\\Delta$$\\leq$0.79pp at 50\\%, s42), while magnitude-trained BCE achieves 28.13$\\pm$0.20. Gradient coupling remains necessary at the discovery stage.\n\nWe instantiate a lightweight predictor (75.9M params, $<$1\\% of base) trained with forward KL and Gumbel-Sigmoid relaxation~\\cite{jang2017gumbel} on LLaMA-3.1-8B~\\cite{grattafiori2024llama3}.\nAt 30\\% sparsity under uniform allocation, the predictor reduces TEAL's perplexity by 19\\% (8.81 vs.\\ 10.82) with stable downstream (MMLU 40.7$\\pm$1.3\\%, 3-seed); global top-$k$ amplifies cross-seed variance ($7.3\\times$).\nAt 50\\%, the perplexity advantage widens (50\\% over TEAL), but downstream reasoning collapses regardless of method.\n\nOn Mistral-7B-v0.3~\\cite{jiang2023mistral}, KL outperforms TEAL on 5 of 6 downstream tasks at 30\\% (ARC +9.0pp, HellaSwag +8.8pp, WG +5.6pp; GSM8K reverses); on Qwen-2.5-14B~\\cite{qwen2024qwen25}, PPL generalizes but reasoning declines sharply (GSM8K $-$20pp) and MMLU reverses (63.7\\% vs.\\ TEAL's 71.8\\%), suggesting task-and-scale-dependent effects.\nThis paper is primarily an empirical study: we do not propose a new architecture or algorithm, but rather characterize how the training objective shapes sparsity prediction quality, and analyze interactions between allocation strategy, sparsity level, and model scale. Wall-clock speedup requires sparse kernel support not addressed here (Appendix~\\ref{app:latency}).\n\nOur contributions are:\n\\begin{itemize}[nosep,leftmargin=*]\n\\item A target-swap ablation confirms that KL's discovered mask targets drive inference-stage quality; gradient coupling remains necessary at the discovery stage (\\S\\ref{sec:mask_analysis}).\n\\item These mask patterns render post-hoc compensation (+67.2M params) redundant at both tested sparsity levels (\\S\\ref{sec:isocompute}).\n\\item Analysis of allocation-to-downstream interactions at 7--14B: non-monotonic allocation preference at 8B and scale-dependent effects at 14B (\\S\\ref{sec:scaling}).\n\\end{itemize}\n",
    "structuredPatch": [
      {
        "oldStart": 1,
        "oldLines": 6,
        "newStart": 1,
        "newLines": 7,
        "lines": [

          "+While our distillation objective is architecture-agnostic, SwiGLU's gated activation makes sparsity prediction particularly challenging: neuron importance depends on both gate and up-projection values, creating a non-trivial prediction target that magnitude-based methods handle suboptimally.",
          " Learned sparsity predictors~\\cite{liu2023dejavu,gautam2026fastforward} exploit this property by training small networks to anticipate which neurons are important, but they universally rely on binary cross-entropy (BCE) loss, treating each neuron as an independent classification target.",
          " Yet at inference, sparsity budgets are allocated globally: the TEAL (training-free activation sparsity;~\\citealp{liu2025teal}) top-$k$ mechanism ranks all neurons across all layers and retains only those above a model-wide threshold.",

    "oldString": "KL-derived masks diverge 46--68\\% from magnitude-based masks (depending on sparsity level); a target-swap ablation confirms that these mask targets, discovered through KL's coupled gradients, are the proximate quality driver when transferred to simpler objectives.",
    "newString": "KL-derived masks diverge 46--68\\% from magnitude-based masks (depending on sparsity level); a target-swap ablation confirms that these mask targets, discovered through KL's coupled gradients, are the proximate quality driver when transferred to simpler objectives.\nPer-layer intermediate coupling (MSE) actively degrades below training-free baselines (PPL 20.4 vs.\\ 10.8 at 30\\%), revealing that \\emph{coupling alignment}---not coupling per se---is the decisive factor.",
    "originalFile": "Learned sparsity predictors for SwiGLU (Swish-Gated Linear Unit) LLMs universally use binary cross-entropy (BCE), training each neuron independently, yet inference allocates budgets globally via top-$k$ ranking.\nWe train predictors with Kullback-Leibler (KL) divergence, coupling all mask decisions through the output distribution.\nAt 30\\% sparsity on LLaMA-3.1-8B, the KL predictor ($<$1\\% of base parameters) reduces perplexity by 19\\% relative to TEAL with moderate downstream degradation, and renders post-hoc compensation (+67.2M params) redundant in prediction quality.\nKL-derived masks diverge 46--68\\% from magnitude-based masks (depending on sparsity level); a target-swap ablation confirms that these mask targets, discovered through KL's coupled gradients, are the proximate quality driver when transferred to simpler objectives.\nPreliminary single-seed evaluation on Mistral-7B-v0.3 and cross-scale evaluation on Qwen-2.5-14B (uniform allocation) reveal consistent PPL improvements but scale-dependent downstream effects.\nAt 50\\% sparsity the perplexity advantage widens, though downstream reasoning collapses to near-random regardless of method, revealing a capacity cliff.\n",
    "structuredPatch": [
      {
        "oldStart": 2,
        "oldLines": 5,
        "newStart": 2,
        "newLines": 6,
        "lines": [
          " We train predictors with Kullback-Leibler (KL) divergence, coupling all mask decisions through the output distribution.",
          " At 30\\% sparsity on LLaMA-3.1-8B, the KL predictor ($<$1\\% of base parameters) reduces perplexity by 19\\% relative to TEAL with moderate downstream degradation, and renders post-hoc compensation (+67.2M params) redundant in prediction quality.",
          " KL-derived masks diverge 46--68\\% from magnitude-based masks (depending on sparsity level); a target-swap ablation confirms that these mask targets, discovered through KL's coupled gradients, are the proximate quality driver when transferred to simpler objectives.",
          "+Per-layer intermediate coupling (MSE) actively degrades below training-free baselines (PPL 20.4 vs.\\ 10.8 at 30\\%), revealing that \\emph{coupling alignment}---not coupling per se---is the decisive factor.",
          " Preliminary single-seed evaluation on Mistral-7B-v0.3 and cross-scale evaluation on Qwen-2.5-14B (uniform allocation) reveal consistent PPL improvements but scale-dependent downstream effects.",
          " At 50\\% sparsity the perplexity advantage widens, though downstream reasoning collapses to near-random regardless of method, revealing a capacity cliff."
        ]

{
  "recorded_role": "user",
  "parentUuid": "c898d552-c740-42c9-b3e8-f5863082e863",
  "isSidechain": false,
  "promptId": "9152bf77-e65d-46e2-8d31-96a27284777d",

  "uuid": "0360bec2-9510-43a7-99ba-07623db9b9aa",
  "toolUseResult": {
    "filePath": "/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0509_14/projects/distill_sparse_swiglu/paper/introduction.tex",
    "oldString": "While our distillation objective is architecture-agnostic, SwiGLU's gated activation makes sparsity prediction particularly challenging: neuron importance depends on both gate and up-projection values, creating a non-trivial prediction target that magnitude-based methods handle suboptimally.\nLearned sparsity predictors",
    "newString": "SwiGLU's gated activation makes prediction challenging: importance depends on both gate and up-projection values, a target that magnitude-based methods handle suboptimally.\nLearned sparsity predictors",
    "originalFile": "\\section{Introduction}\n\nSwiGLU feed-forward networks~\\cite{shazeer2020glu} in modern LLMs exhibit high contextual sparsity: at any given token, a large fraction of FFN neurons contribute negligibly to the output.\nWhile our distillation objective is architecture-agnostic, SwiGLU's gated activation makes sparsity prediction particularly challenging: neuron importance depends on both gate and up-projection values, creating a non-trivial prediction target that magnitude-based methods handle suboptimally.\nLearned sparsity predictors~\\cite{liu2023dejavu,gautam2026fastforward} exploit this property by training small networks to anticipate which neurons are important, but they universally rely on binary cross-entropy (BCE) loss, treating each neuron as an independent classification target.\nYet at inference, sparsity budgets are allocated globally: the TEAL (training-free activation sparsity;~\\citealp{liu2025teal}) top-$k$ mechanism ranks all neurons across all layers and retains only those above a model-wide threshold.\nThis mismatch between per-neuron training and global inference allocation has not been formally analyzed.\n\nGradient analysis reveals a structural asymmetry: BCE's gradient $\\partial \\mathcal{L}_{\\text{BCE}} / \\partial s_j = \\sigma(s_j) - m_j^*$ depends only on neuron $j$, while under KL divergence, the gradient couples each neuron to all others through the downstream Jacobian (\\S\\ref{sec:method:gradient}).\nKL's coupled gradients discover neuron importance patterns that diverge 68\\% from magnitude-based masks (per-layer Jaccard 32\\%).\nA target-swap ablation confirms that these discovered mask targets are the proximate quality driver at inference: BCE trained on KL-derived targets reproduces KL quality (max $\\Delta$$\\leq$0.79pp at 50\\%, s42), while magnitude-trained BCE achieves 28.13$\\pm$0.20. Gradient coupling remains necessary at the discovery stage.\n\nWe instantiate a lightweight predictor (75.9M params, $<$1\\% of base) trained with forward KL and Gumbel-Sigmoid relaxation~\\cite{jang2017gumbel} on LLaMA-3.1-8B~\\cite{grattafiori2024llama3}.\nAt 30\\% sparsity under uniform allocation, the predictor reduces TEAL's perplexity by 19\\% (8.81 vs.\\ 10.82) with stable downstream (MMLU 40.7$\\pm$1.3\\%, 3-seed); global top-$k$ amplifies cross-seed variance ($7.3\\times$).\nAt 50\\%, the perplexity advantage widens (50\\% over TEAL), but downstream reasoning collapses regardless of method.\n\nOn Mistral-7B-v0.3~\\cite{jiang2023mistral}, KL outperforms TEAL on 5 of 6 downstream tasks at 30\\% (ARC +9.0pp, HellaSwag +8.8pp, WG +5.6pp; GSM8K reverses); on Qwen-2.5-14B~\\cite{qwen2024qwen25}, PPL generalizes but reasoning declines sharply (GSM8K $-$20pp) and MMLU reverses (63.7\\% vs.\\ TEAL's 71.8\\%), suggesting task-and-scale-dependent effects.\nThis paper is primarily an empirical study: we do not propose a new architecture or algorithm, but rather characterize how the training objective shapes sparsity prediction quality, and analyze interactions between allocation strategy, sparsity level, and model scale. Wall-clock speedup requires sparse kernel support not addressed here (Appendix~\\ref{app:latency}).\n\nOur contributions are:\n\\begin{itemize}[nosep,leftmargin=*]\n\\item A target-swap ablation confirms that KL's discovered mask targets drive inference-stage quality; gradient coupling remains necessary at the discovery stage (\\S\\ref{sec:mask_analysis}).\n\\item These mask patterns render post-hoc compensation (+67.2M params) redundant at both tested sparsity levels (\\S\\ref{sec:isocompute}).\n\\item We identify \\emph{coupling alignment} as the key factor: per-layer output coupling (MSE) degrades below training-free baselines, while end-to-end distributional coupling (KL) discovers composable masks---reframing the taxonomy from a granularity hierarchy into an alignment distinction (\\S\\ref{sec:method:taxonomy}).\n\\item Analysis of allocation-to-downstream interactions at 7--14B: non-monotonic allocation preference at 8B and scale-dependent effects at 14B (\\S\\ref{sec:scaling}).\n\\end{itemize}\n",
    "structuredPatch": [
      {
        "oldStart": 1,
        "oldLines": 7,
        "newStart": 1,
        "newLines": 7,
        "lines": [
          " \\section{Introduction}",
          " ",

          "-While our distillation objective is architecture-agnostic, SwiGLU's gated activation makes sparsity prediction particularly challenging: neuron importance depends on both gate and up-projection values, creating a non-trivial prediction target that magnitude-based methods handle suboptimally.",

          " Yet at inference, sparsity budgets are allocated globally: the TEAL (training-free activation sparsity;~\\citealp{liu2025teal}) top-$k$ mechanism ranks all neurons across all layers and retains only those above a model-wide threshold.",

          "-Per-layer output matching (MSE) couples mask gradients through layer-level reconstruction, yet at 30\\% sparsity its PPL (20.4) is nearly double the training-free baseline (TEAL, 10.8), and at 50\\% it collapses (PPL 229; \\S\\ref{sec:experiments}).",
          "-The failure confirms that the decisive factor is not coupling itself but \\emph{coupling alignment}: only end-to-end distributional matching discovers masks that compose well across layers. % exp_id: mse30_uniform_s42, mse50_uniform_s42",
          "+Per-layer output matching (MSE) couples gradients within each layer, yet its PPL (20.4 at 30\\%; 229 at 50\\%) is worse than the training-free baseline (TEAL, 10.8), confirming that \\emph{coupling alignment}---not coupling per se---is decisive: only end-to-end distributional matching discovers composable masks (\\S\\ref{sec:experiments}). % exp_id: mse30_uniform_s42, mse50_uniform_s42",
          " ",
          " \\begin{figure}[t]",
          " \\centering"
        ]
      }

    "newString": "Per-layer coupling (MSE) degrades below training-free baselines (PPL 20.4 vs.\\ 10.8), revealing \\emph{coupling alignment}---not coupling per se---as decisive.",
    "originalFile": "Learned sparsity predictors for SwiGLU (Swish-Gated Linear Unit) LLMs universally use binary cross-entropy (BCE), training each neuron independently, yet inference allocates budgets globally via top-$k$ ranking.\nWe train predictors with Kullback-Leibler (KL) divergence, coupling all mask decisions through the output distribution.\nAt 30\\% sparsity on LLaMA-3.1-8B, the KL predictor ($<$1\\% of base parameters) reduces perplexity by 19\\% relative to TEAL with moderate downstream degradation, and renders post-hoc compensation (+67.2M params) redundant in prediction quality.\nKL-derived masks diverge 46--68\\% from magnitude-based masks (depending on sparsity level); a target-swap ablation confirms that these mask targets, discovered through KL's coupled gradients, are the proximate quality driver when transferred to simpler objectives.\nPer-layer intermediate coupling (MSE) actively degrades below training-free baselines (PPL 20.4 vs.\\ 10.8 at 30\\%), revealing that \\emph{coupling alignment}---not coupling per se---is the decisive factor.\nPreliminary single-seed evaluation on Mistral-7B-v0.3 and cross-scale evaluation on Qwen-2.5-14B (uniform allocation) reveal consistent PPL improvements but scale-dependent downstream effects.\nAt 50\\% sparsity the perplexity advantage widens, though downstream reasoning collapses to near-random regardless of method, revealing a capacity cliff.\n",
    "structuredPatch": [
      {
        "oldStart": 2,
        "oldLines": 6,
        "newStart": 2,

          " KL-derived masks diverge 46--68\\% from magnitude-based masks (depending on sparsity level); a target-swap ablation confirms that these mask targets, discovered through KL's coupled gradients, are the proximate quality driver when transferred to simpler objectives.",
          "-Per-layer intermediate coupling (MSE) actively degrades below training-free baselines (PPL 20.4 vs.\\ 10.8 at 30\\%), revealing that \\emph{coupling alignment}---not coupling per se---is the decisive factor.",
          "+Per-layer coupling (MSE) degrades below training-free baselines (PPL 20.4 vs.\\ 10.8), revealing \\emph{coupling alignment}---not coupling per se---as decisive.",

      "type": "text",
      "file": {
        "filePath": "/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0509_14/projects/distill_sparse_swiglu/paper/abstract.tex",
        "content": "Learned sparsity predictors for SwiGLU (Swish-Gated Linear Unit) LLMs universally use binary cross-entropy (BCE), training each neuron independently, yet inference allocates budgets globally via top-$k$ ranking.\nWe train predictors with Kullback-Leibler (KL) divergence, coupling all mask decisions through the output distribution.\nAt 30\\% sparsity on LLaMA-3.1-8B, the KL predictor ($<$1\\% of base parameters) reduces perplexity by 19\\% relative to TEAL with moderate downstream degradation, and renders post-hoc compensation (+67.2M params) redundant in prediction quality.\nKL-derived masks diverge 46--68\\% from magnitude-based masks (depending on sparsity level); a target-swap ablation confirms that these mask targets, discovered through KL's coupled gradients, are the proximate quality driver when transferred to simpler objectives.\nPer-layer coupling (MSE) degrades below training-free baselines (PPL 20.4 vs.\\ 10.8), revealing \\emph{coupling alignment}---not coupling per se---as decisive.\nPreliminary single-seed evaluation on Mistral-7B-v0.3 and cross-scale evaluation on Qwen-2.5-14B (uniform allocation) reveal consistent PPL improvements but scale-dependent downstream effects.\nAt 50\\% sparsity the perplexity advantage widens, though downstream reasoning collapses to near-random regardless of method, revealing a capacity cliff.\n",
        "numLines": 8,
        "startLine": 1,
        "totalLines": 8
      }
    },
    "displayPath": "../../../paper/abstract.tex"
  },
  "type": "attachment",

{
  "parentUuid": "a995e9f6-df37-4918-bd90-d8031a4f8e02",
  "isSidechain": false,
  "attachment": {
    "type": "file",
    "filename": "/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0509_14/projects/distill_sparse_swiglu/paper/method.tex",
    "content": {
      "type": "text",
      "file": {
        "filePath": "/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0509_14/projects/distill_sparse_swiglu/paper/method.tex",
        "content": "\\section{Method}\n\\label{sec:method}\n\nWe present a KL distillation framework for training contextual sparsity predictors in SwiGLU-based language models (Figure~\\ref{fig:overview}).\nThe method replaces per-neuron binary classification (BCE) with an output-distribution objective that couples all pruning decisions through the model's forward pass and achieves competitive performance without auxiliary error compensation networks at inference.\n\n\\subsection{Problem Formulation}\n\\label{sec:method:formulation}\n\nConsider a pretrained transformer with $L$ layers, each containing a SwiGLU feed-forward network~\\cite{shazeer2020glu}.\nFor layer $\\ell$, the FFN computes:\n\\begin{align}\n\\mathbf{y}^{(\\ell)} = \\bigl(\\sigma(\\mathbf{x}^{(\\ell)} \\mathbf{W}_g^{(\\ell)}) \\odot \\mathbf{x}^{(\\ell)} \\mathbf{W}_u^{(\\ell)}\\bigr) \\mathbf{W}_d^{(\\ell)},\n\\label{eq:swiglu}\n\\end{align}\nwhere $\\mathbf{x}^{(\\ell)} \\in \\mathbb{R}^{d}$ is the hidden state after attention ($d{=}4096$), $\\sigma$ denotes SiLU, $\\mathbf{W}_g^{(\\ell)}, \\mathbf{W}_u^{(\\ell)} \\in \\mathbb{R}^{d \\times d_{\\text{ff}}}$ and $\\mathbf{W}_d^{(\\ell)} \\in \\mathbb{R}^{d_{\\text{ff}} \\times d}$ are the gate, up-projection, and down-projection matrices, and $d_{\\text{ff}}{=}14{,}336$.\n\nDefine the gate activation $\\mathbf{g}^{(\\ell)} = \\sigma(\\mathbf{x}^{(\\ell)} \\mathbf{W}_g^{(\\ell)}) \\in \\mathbb{R}^{d_{\\text{ff}}}$.\nEach element $g_j^{(\\ell)}$ determines the contribution of neuron $j$ to the output.\nWe introduce a binary mask $\\mathbf{m}^{(\\ell)} \\in \\{0,1\\}^{d_{\\text{ff}}}$ that zeros out pruned neurons:\n\\begin{align}\n\\mathbf{y}_{\\text{sparse}}^{(\\ell)} = \\bigl(\\mathbf{m}^{(\\ell)} \\odot \\mathbf{g}^{(\\ell)} \\odot \\mathbf{x}^{(\\ell)} \\mathbf{W}_u^{(\\ell)}\\bigr) \\mathbf{W}_d^{(\\ell)}.\n\\label{eq:sparse_ffn}\n\\end{align}\nOur goal is to learn a mask predictor $f_\\theta^{(\\ell)}(\\mathbf{x}^{(\\ell)}) \\to \\mathbf{s}^{(\\ell)} \\in (0,1)^{d_{\\text{ff}}}$ that produces per-neuron importance scores from $\\mathbf{x}^{(\\ell)}$.\nAt inference, the scores are fed to a global top-$k$ allocation mechanism~\\cite{liu2025teal} that distributes the sparsity budget across layers non-uniformly, retaining the $(1{-}\\rho) \\times L \\times d_{\\text{ff}}$ highest-scoring neurons model-wide for a target sparsity ratio $\\rho$.\n\n\n\\subsection{Predictor Architecture}\n\\label{sec:method:predictor}\n\nEach layer $\\ell$ has an independent two-layer MLP predictor:\n\\begin{align}\nf_\\theta^{(\\ell)}(\\mathbf{x}^{(\\ell)}) = \\text{GELU}(\\mathbf{x}^{(\\ell)} \\mathbf{W}_1^{(\\ell)}) \\, \\mathbf{W}_2^{(\\ell)},\n\\label{eq:predictor}\n\\end{align}\nwhere $\\mathbf{W}_1^{(\\ell)} \\in \\mathbb{R}^{d \\times d_b}$ and $\\mathbf{W}_2^{(\\ell)} \\in \\mathbb{R}^{d_b \\times d_{\\text{ff}}}$ with bottleneck dimension $d_b{=}128$.\nThe predictor takes $\\mathbf{x}^{(\\ell)}$ (the hidden state after attention, before the FFN) and outputs $d_{\\text{ff}}$-dimensional logits $\\mathbf{s}^{(\\ell)}$, from which the mask is derived.\n\nEach predictor has $(4096 \\times 128) + (128 \\times 14336) = 2.37\\text{M}$ parameters.\nWith 32 independent predictors (one per layer), the total overhead is 75.9M parameters, less than 1\\% of the 8B base model.\nThe predictors operate independently across layers, requiring no cross-layer communication at inference.\n\n\n\\subsection{Gumbel-Sigmoid Relaxation}\n\\label{sec:method:gumbel}\n\nThe hard mask $m_j = \\mathbf{1}[s_j > \\text{threshold}]$ is non-differentiable, preventing gradient flow from the KL objective through the mask to the predictor parameters.\nWe apply Gumbel-Sigmoid relaxation~\\cite{maddison2017concrete,louizos2018l0,jang2017gumbel} to produce differentiable soft masks:\n\\begin{align}\n\\tilde{m}_j = \\sigma_\\tau\\!\\left(\\log \\frac{s_j}{1 - s_j} + g_1 - g_2\\right), \\quad g_1, g_2 \\sim \\text{Gumbel}(0,1),\n\\label{eq:gumbel}\n\\end{align}\nwhere $\\sigma_\\tau(x) = \\text{sigmoid}(x / \\tau)$ and $\\tau$ is a temperature parameter.\nThe Gumbel noise provides stochastic exploration of mask configurations during training, while the temperature controls sharpness: as $\\tau \\to 0$, $\\tilde{m}_j$ approaches a binary decision.\n\nWe anneal $\\tau$ linearly from 1.0 to 0.1 over training.\nAt high temperature, the soft mask permits gradient flow through all neurons for broad exploration.\nAs temperature decreases, the mask concentrates on confident decisions, approximating the hard allocation used at inference.\n\n\\paragraph{Comparison with Straight-Through Estimation.}\nThe straight-through estimator (STE)~\\cite{bengio2013ste} applies a hard threshold in the forward pass and passes gradients through unchanged ($\\partial m / \\partial s = 1$).\nIn our setting, STE produces discontinuous gradient signals: small parameter changes can flip many neurons simultaneously. The resulting oscillations in the penalty weight prevent convergence to the target sparsity.\nGumbel relaxation eliminates this pathology and contributes a 33.8\\% improvement (PPL 15.77 $\\to$ 11.79 at 50\\% sparsity), as we verify empirically in \\S\\ref{sec:experiments}.\n\n\n\\subsection{KL Divergence Training Objective}\n\\label{sec:method:kl}\n\nLet $\\mathbf{z}_{\\text{dense}}$ denote the output logits from a full dense forward pass, and $\\mathbf{z}_{\\text{sparse}}$ the logits from a forward pass with predictor-generated soft masks $\\{\\tilde{\\mathbf{m}}^{(\\ell)}\\}_\\ell$ applied at every layer.\nWe minimize the forward KL divergence between the dense and sparse output distributions:\n\\begin{align}\n\\mathcal{L}_{\\text{KL}} = D_{\\text{KL}}(p_{\\text{dense}} \\,\\|\\, p_{\\text{sparse}}) = \\sum_{v} p_{\\text{dense}}^{(v)} \\log \\frac{p_{\\text{dense}}^{(v)}}{p_{\\text{sparse}}^{(v)}},\n\\label{eq:kl_loss}\n\\end{align}\nwhere $p_{\\text{dense}} = \\text{softmax}(\\mathbf{z}_{\\text{dense}})$ and $p_{\\text{sparse}} = \\text{softmax}(\\mathbf{z}_{\\text{sparse}})$, summed over the vocabulary $v$ and averaged over sequence positions.\nThe base model is frozen; only the predictor parameters receive gradients.\n\nWe use forward KL (mass-covering) rather than reverse KL, as the goal is to preserve the dense model's primary predictions rather than explore alternative modes; \\S\\ref{sec:experiments} validates this choice with ablations over divergence direction (forward KL outperforms JSD by 6.4\\% and reverse KL by 8.6\\%).\nNo temperature scaling ($T{=}1$) is applied~\\cite{hinton2015distilling}.\n\n\n\\subsection{Adaptive Penalty Controller}\n\\label{sec:method:lagrangian}\n\nWe enforce a model-level sparsity target $\\rho$ via constrained optimization with $\\bar{s} = \\frac{1}{L} \\sum_\\ell \\frac{1}{d_{\\text{ff}}} \\sum_j \\tilde{m}_j^{(\\ell)}$ denoting the average retained fraction:\n\\begin{align}\n\\min_\\theta \\; \\mathcal{L}_{\\text{KL}} \\quad \\text{s.t.} \\quad \\bar{s} = 1 - \\rho.\n\\label{eq:constrained}\n\\end{align}\nWe relax this via an adaptive penalty controller:\n\\begin{align}\n\\mathcal{L} = \\mathcal{L}_{\\text{KL}} + \\lambda \\cdot \\text{clamp}(|\\bar{s} - (1 - \\rho)| - \\epsilon, \\min{=}0)^2,\n\\label{eq:lagrangian}\n\\end{align}\nwhere $\\epsilon = 0.02$ is a deadzone margin. The penalty weight $\\lambda$ is updated via geometric doubling (doubled when sparsity exceeds target, halved otherwise), clamped to $[\\lambda_{\\text{floor}}, \\lambda_{\\max}]$, reaching target sparsity within 40--110 steps across all tested regimes (Appendix~\\ref{app:lagrangian}, Figure~\\ref{fig:lagrangian_dynamics}).\n\n\n\\subsection{Gradient Analysis: BCE vs.\\ KL}\n\\label{sec:method:gradient}\n\nTo build intuition for how KL distillation discovers neuron importance patterns that diverge from magnitude-based masks (\\S\\ref{sec:mask_analysis}), we contrast the gradient structure of the two objectives. This analysis is suggestive; \\S\\ref{sec:mask_analysis} provides evidence that the coupled gradient discovers mask targets that yield lower perplexity, with mask quality driving inference-stage quality.\n\nUnder BCE, the predictor trains against oracle masks $m_j^* = \\mathbf{1}[|g_j| \\geq \\text{top-}k\\text{ threshold}]$ derived from activation magnitudes.\nThe gradient $\\partial \\mathcal{L}_{\\text{BCE}} / \\partial s_j = \\sigma(s_j) - m_j^*$ depends only on neuron $j$; each neuron is trained independently with no inter-neuron coupling.\nUnder KL distillation, the chain rule introduces a downstream Jacobian $\\partial \\mathbf{z}_{\\text{sparse}} / \\partial \\tilde{m}_j^{(\\ell)}$ that couples neuron $j$'s gradient to all other retained neurons in layers $\\ell$ through $L$, because the sparse hidden state propagates through subsequent attention and FFN operations.\nThis coupling allows KL to evaluate each neuron's importance in the context of the full sparse computation, rather than against a fixed magnitude target.\n\nGradient SNR measurements\\footnote{$\\text{SNR}_\\ell = \\|\\mathbb{E}[\\nabla_{\\theta_\\ell} \\mathcal{L}]\\|_2^2 / \\text{Var}[\\nabla_{\\theta_\\ell} \\mathcal{L}]$, expectation over mini-batches.} confirm a structural difference: at step 300 (of 1000), KL achieves $5.14\\times$ higher SNR than BCE ($5.877{\\times}10^{-3}$ vs.\\ $1.144{\\times}10^{-3}$, LLaMA-3.1-8B, C4; Appendix~\\ref{app:snr_extended}).\nWe note that the SNR trajectory is non-monotonic: BCE briefly surpasses KL during steps 100--250 before KL establishes sustained advantage from step 300 onward (Appendix~\\ref{app:snr_extended}).\nThe coupled gradient provides a mechanism by which KL can discover mask patterns that diverge from magnitude ranking; the target-swap ablation in \\S\\ref{sec:mask_analysis} confirms that the discovered mask targets, not the coupling mechanism itself, drive inference-stage quality.\n\n\\subsection{Two-Dimensional Taxonomy of Sparsity Prediction Methods}\n\\label{sec:method:taxonomy}\n\nWe organize prior work along two axes: \\emph{correction granularity} (how the mask adapts to input) and \\emph{training signal coupling} (whether the objective captures inter-neuron dependencies).\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\caption{Taxonomy of learned sparsity prediction methods along correction granularity (rows) and training signal coupling (columns). Our method fills the previously unexplored Per-token $\\times$ Coupled cell.}\n\\label{tab:taxonomy}\n\\resizebox{\\columnwidth}{!}{\n\\begin{tabular}{lccc}\n\\toprule\n & Decomposed (BCE) & Intermediate (MSE) & Coupled (KL) \\\\\n\\midrule\nStatic & --- & --- & SPON \\\\\nPer-token & DejaVu, FastForward & ShadowLLM, D2DMoE & \\textbf{Ours} \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}}\n\\end{table}\n\n\\paragraph{Correction granularity.}\n\\emph{Static} methods~\\cite{xu2026spon} learn a fixed bias vector per layer that does not depend on the input token.\n\\emph{Per-token} methods~\\cite{liu2023dejavu,gautam2026fastforward,akhauri2024shadowllm,szatkowski2024d2dmoe} produce a unique mask for each input position, conditioning on the token's hidden state at each layer.\n\n\\paragraph{Training signal coupling.}\n\\emph{Decomposed} objectives (BCE) treat each neuron independently, optimizing per-element classification against an oracle mask.\n\\emph{Intermediate} objectives (MSE on hidden states) capture local coupling within a single layer but not end-to-end output effects.\n\\emph{Coupled} objectives (KL on output distribution) propagate gradients through the full forward pass; each mask decision's gradient reflects its joint effect with all other masks on the final distribution.\n\nSPON~\\cite{xu2026spon} distills a per-layer bias via KL but cannot adapt to per-input neuron importance (static $\\times$ coupled).\nDejaVu~\\cite{liu2023dejavu} and FastForward~\\cite{gautam2026fastforward} achieve per-token granularity but use BCE, where each neuron's gradient is independent of other mask states.\nD2DMoE~\\cite{szatkowski2024d2dmoe} provides intermediate coupling (MSE on hidden states) but not end-to-end output effects.\nOur method combines per-token prediction with coupled KL training (Table~\\ref{tab:taxonomy}), propagating gradients through all downstream layers.\nPer-layer output matching (MSE) couples gradients within each layer, yet its PPL (20.4 at 30\\%; 229 at 50\\%) is worse than the training-free baseline (TEAL, 10.8), confirming that \\emph{coupling alignment}---not coupling per se---is decisive: only end-to-end distributional matching discovers composable masks (\\S\\ref{sec:experiments}). % exp_id: mse30_uniform_s42, mse50_uniform_s42\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{fig1_overview.pdf}\n\\caption{Method overview: a lightweight per-layer predictor produces importance scores trained via KL divergence with Gumbel-Sigmoid relaxation. At inference, scores feed TEAL's global top-$k$ allocation without compensation networks.}\n\\label{fig:overview}\n\\end{figure}\n",
        "numLines": 154,
        "startLine": 1,
        "totalLines": 154
      }
    },

  },

{

    "content": {
      "type": "text",
      "file": {
        "filePath": "/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0509_14/projects/distill_sparse_swiglu/paper/introduction.tex",
        "content": "\\section{Introduction}\n\nSwiGLU feed-forward networks~\\cite{shazeer2020glu} in modern LLMs exhibit high contextual sparsity: at any given token, a large fraction of FFN neurons contribute negligibly to the output.\nSwiGLU's gated activation makes prediction challenging: importance depends on both gate and up-projection values, a target that magnitude-based methods handle suboptimally.\nLearned sparsity predictors~\\cite{liu2023dejavu,gautam2026fastforward} exploit this property by training small networks to anticipate which neurons are important, but they universally rely on binary cross-entropy (BCE) loss, treating each neuron as an independent classification target.\nYet at inference, sparsity budgets are allocated globally: the TEAL (training-free activation sparsity;~\\citealp{liu2025teal}) top-$k$ mechanism ranks all neurons across all layers and retains only those above a model-wide threshold.\nThis mismatch between per-neuron training and global inference allocation has not been formally analyzed.\n\nGradient analysis reveals a structural asymmetry: BCE's gradient $\\partial \\mathcal{L}_{\\text{BCE}} / \\partial s_j = \\sigma(s_j) - m_j^*$ depends only on neuron $j$, while under KL divergence, the gradient couples each neuron to all others through the downstream Jacobian (\\S\\ref{sec:method:gradient}).\nKL's coupled gradients discover neuron importance patterns that diverge 68\\% from magnitude-based masks (per-layer Jaccard 32\\%).\nA target-swap ablation confirms that these discovered mask targets are the proximate quality driver at inference: BCE trained on KL-derived targets reproduces KL quality (max $\\Delta$$\\leq$0.79pp at 50\\%, s42), while magnitude-trained BCE achieves 28.13$\\pm$0.20. Gradient coupling remains necessary at the discovery stage.\n\nWe instantiate a lightweight predictor (75.9M params, $<$1\\% of base) trained with forward KL and Gumbel-Sigmoid relaxation~\\cite{jang2017gumbel} on LLaMA-3.1-8B~\\cite{grattafiori2024llama3}.\nAt 30\\% sparsity under uniform allocation, the predictor reduces TEAL's perplexity by 19\\% (8.81 vs.\\ 10.82) with stable downstream (MMLU 40.7$\\pm$1.3\\%, 3-seed); global top-$k$ amplifies cross-seed variance ($7.3\\times$).\nAt 50\\%, the perplexity advantage widens (50\\% over TEAL), but downstream reasoning collapses regardless of method.\n\nOn Mistral-7B-v0.3~\\cite{jiang2023mistral}, KL outperforms TEAL on 5 of 6 downstream tasks at 30\\% (ARC +9.0pp, HellaSwag +8.8pp, WG +5.6pp; GSM8K reverses); on Qwen-2.5-14B~\\cite{qwen2024qwen25}, PPL generalizes but reasoning declines sharply (GSM8K $-$20pp) and MMLU reverses (63.7\\% vs.\\ TEAL's 71.8\\%), suggesting task-and-scale-dependent effects.\nThis paper is primarily an empirical study: we do not propose a new architecture or algorithm, but rather characterize how the training objective shapes sparsity prediction quality, and analyze interactions between allocation strategy, sparsity level, and model scale. Wall-clock speedup requires sparse kernel support not addressed here (Appendix~\\ref{app:latency}).\n\nOur contributions are:\n\\begin{itemize}[nosep,leftmargin=*]\n\\item A target-swap ablation confirms that KL's discovered mask targets drive inference-stage quality; gradient coupling remains necessary at the discovery stage (\\S\\ref{sec:mask_analysis}).\n\\item These mask patterns render post-hoc compensation (+67.2M params) redundant at both tested sparsity levels (\\S\\ref{sec:isocompute}).\n\\item Per-layer coupling (MSE) degrades below training-free baselines; only end-to-end KL discovers composable masks, identifying \\emph{coupling alignment} as the decisive factor (\\S\\ref{sec:method:taxonomy}).\n\\item Analysis of allocation-to-downstream interactions at 7--14B: non-monotonic allocation preference at 8B and scale-dependent effects at 14B (\\S\\ref{sec:scaling}).\n\\end{itemize}\n",
        "numLines": 27,
        "startLine": 1,
        "totalLines": 27
      }
    },

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_013PCxLs3bsFqvZz7fMyGntb`</summary>

```text
1	\section{Experiments}
2	\label{sec:experiments}
3	
4	\subsection{Experimental Setup}
5	
6	\paragraph{Model and Predictor.}
7	We evaluate on LLaMA-3.1-8B~\cite{grattafiori2024llama3}, which uses SwiGLU FFN blocks~\cite{shazeer2020glu} with $d_{\text{model}}=4096$ and $d_{\text{ff}}=14336$ across 32 layers.
8	The sparsity predictor is a two-layer MLP with 2.37M parameters per layer (75.9M total), implemented as Linear($4096 \to 128$) + GELU + Linear($128 \to 14336$), trained to produce per-neuron importance scores that minimize output-distribution divergence under sparsity.
9	
10	\paragraph{Training.}
11	All predictors are trained for 1000 steps on WikiText-103 with sequence length 2048 and batch size 4 on a single NVIDIA RTX PRO 6000 Blackwell Server Edition GPU (96\,GB), using AdamW~\cite{loshchilov2019adamw} with learning rate $1 \times 10^{-3}$, weight decay $5 \times 10^{-2}$, and cosine decay.
12	The KL predictor uses Gumbel-Sigmoid relaxation~\cite{jang2017gumbel} with temperature $\tau=1.0$ annealed to $0.1$ over training.
13	
14	\paragraph{Evaluation.}
15	We report perplexity on WikiText-2~\cite{merity2017pointer} (seq\_len=2048) and downstream accuracy on ARC-Challenge (25-shot), WinoGrande (5-shot), MMLU (5-shot), GSM8K (8-shot), HellaSwag (10-shot), and PIQA (0-shot).
16	
17	\paragraph{Inference Protocol.}
18	At inference, the predictor produces per-neuron scores that are passed to TEAL's global top-$k$ allocation mechanism~\cite{liu2025teal}, which distributes the sparsity budget across layers non-uniformly.
19	We term this \textit{predictor-TEAL}: the predictor replaces TEAL's magnitude-based scoring while retaining its allocation strategy.
20	
21	\paragraph{Baselines.}
22	We compare against: (1) \textbf{Vanilla TEAL}~\cite{liu2025teal}, which uses activation magnitudes directly\footnote{TEAL is deterministic; single-run evaluation suffices. KL/BCE predictors require multi-seed evaluation due to random initialization.}; (2) \textbf{BCE predictor} (DejaVu-style~\cite{liu2023dejavu}), trained with per-neuron binary cross-entropy on magnitude-based oracle masks for 1000 and 2700 steps; and (3) \textbf{Uniform allocation}, which distributes the sparsity budget equally across layers.
23	
24	
25	\subsection{Main Results}
26	
27	Table~\ref{tab:main} presents the primary comparison. Uniform allocation preserves downstream performance best; global allocation is analyzed to understand allocation-quality tradeoffs (\S\ref{sec:scaling}).
28	At 30\% sparsity, KL distillation under uniform allocation achieves PPL 8.81$\pm$0.02 (3-seed), reducing TEAL's perplexity by 19\% (10.82 $\to$ 8.81). % exp_id: bench_kl30_uniform, teal30_uniform
29	HellaSwag (86.3\% retention) and PIQA (92.6\% retention) confirm moderate degradation on non-reasoning tasks (Appendix~\ref{app:downstream}). % exp_id: hellaswag_piqa_kl30_uniform
30	Uniform allocation at 30\% achieves PPL 8.81$\pm$0.02 with stable downstream performance (MMLU 40.7$\pm$1.3\%, 3-seed; full stability analysis in Appendix~\ref{app:stability}), while global top-$k$ exhibits high seed variance (35.7$\pm$9.5\%) from late-layer starvation (L31 retention 28.4\%) interacting with predictor initialization (\S\ref{sec:scaling}). % exp_id: bench_kl30_uniform, kl30_s123_uniform, kl30_s456_uniform, kl30_3seed
31	
32	At 50\% sparsity, the PPL advantage widens: 11.92 vs.\ 23.90 (50\% reduction over TEAL). % exp_id: bench_kl50_uniform, teal50_uniform
33	The predictor generalizes to C4 validation at both 30\% (PPL 16.03$\pm$0.01, 2-seed, global allocation) and 50\% (52.5\% over TEAL; Appendix~\ref{app:ood}).
34	However, all methods exhibit downstream collapse on MMLU (28.1$\pm$0.2\% vs.\ 28.0$\pm$0.6\%, both $\approx$ random, 3-seed) and GSM8K ($<$2.2\%); this is a capacity-limited regime where no allocation strategy can recover. % exp_id: bench_kl50_global, d030_kl50_uniform, multi_seed_kl50_global
35	
36	\begin{table}[t]
37	\centering
38	\caption{Main results on LLaMA-3.1-8B (WikiText-2 PPL$\downarrow$; downstream accuracy$\uparrow$). \textbf{Bold}: best per sparsity. $^\ast$Extended training budget ($2.71\times$ KL step budget), no compensation; training details in \S\ref{sec:isocompute}. $^\dagger$BCE 30\%: TEAL global (2700 steps). $\pm$: 3-seed mean$\pm$std ($s \in \{42, 123, 456\}$); unmarked: seed 42. TEAL uses uniform allocation at all sparsity levels. MSE: per-layer FFN output reconstruction loss (same predictor architecture). Actual achieved sparsity: KL 28.5\%, TEAL 29.3\% at 30\% target; both within 0.5pp at 50\%.}
39	\label{tab:main}
40	\resizebox{\columnwidth}{!}{
41	\begin{tabular}{llccccc}
42	\toprule
43	Sparsity & Method & PPL$\downarrow$ & ARC-c & WG & MMLU & GSM8K \\

63	
64	
65	\subsection{Extended Training Analysis}
66	\label{sec:isocompute}
67	
68	To test whether BCE simply requires more compute, we train it for 2700 steps (2.71$\times$ the KL step budget, approximately equal wall-clock time: $\sim$46 min BCE vs.\ $\sim$45 min KL on RTX PRO 6000).
69	The BCE predictor achieves PPL 28.13$\pm$0.20 (3-seed), still 2.39$\times$ worse than KL at 1000 steps (PPL 11.79). % exp_id: bce50_3seed
70	The difference is systematic, not due to architectural or compute limitations: both predictors use identical architectures and TEAL allocation, yet BCE achieves +7pp oracle F1 (0.613 vs.\ 0.540) with 139\% worse PPL (28.13 vs.\ 11.79). KL's near-random precision (0.51) is consistent with a mask optimized for distributional fidelity rather than magnitude agreement. % exp_id: f1_oracle_analysis, bce_isocompute_s42
71	Coupled training correlates with better output preservation; the target-swap ablation in \S\ref{sec:mask_analysis} confirms that the discovered mask targets, not the coupling mechanism itself, drive inference-stage quality.
72	
73	
74	\subsection{Mask Pattern Analysis}
75	\label{sec:mask_analysis}
76	
77	To understand \emph{what} the KL predictor learns, we compare KL-derived masks against magnitude-based oracle masks (the BCE training targets) at 50\% sparsity on WikiText-2.
78	Per-layer Jaccard similarity averages 32\% at 50\% sparsity (64 samples, 131K tokens), computed per-token (each token's KL and magnitude masks are constructed independently, then Jaccard distances are averaged across tokens): the KL predictor discovers neuron importance patterns that diverge 68\% from activation magnitude, with divergence scaling from 46\% at 30\% to 68\% at 50\% (Figure~\ref{fig:mask_heatmap}).
79	At layer 16, 41\% of neurons fall in disagreement quadrants where KL and magnitude rankings diverge (Appendix~\ref{app:mask_viz}, Figures~\ref{fig:mask_heatmap}--\ref{fig:scatter}).
80	Despite this divergence, KL achieves PPL 11.79 vs.\ BCE's 28.13$\pm$0.20: output-distribution fidelity, not magnitude agreement, produces lower-perplexity masks under global allocation. % exp_id: bench_kl50_global, bce50_3seed, c1_mask_overlap
81	
82	\paragraph{Per-Layer Divergence Structure.}
83	Per-layer Jaccard distance follows a U-shaped pattern (Figure~\ref{fig:mask_heatmap}): at 50\%, middle layers (L14--L17) show lowest divergence ($\sim$0.61), while late layers (L22--L28) peak at $\sim$0.70--0.75. The same structure holds at 30\% (range 0.361--0.600).
84	Late layers are closest to the KL-optimized output distribution, so their backward gradient carries stronger output-sensitivity signal and the predictor deviates most from magnitude ranking there. % exp_id: c1_mask_overlap
85	
86	\paragraph{Target-Swap Ablation: Target Quality vs.\ Gradient Coupling.}
87	To disentangle whether KL's advantage stems from its coupled gradient pathway or from the mask patterns it discovers, we train a standard BCE predictor on KL-derived oracle masks instead of magnitude-based masks (C1 target-swap ablation).
88	Table~\ref{tab:c1} reports results under TEAL uniform allocation at matched sparsity.
89	
90	\begin{table}[t]
91	\centering
92	\caption{Target-swap ablation (C1): BCE on KL-derived masks vs.\ KL predictor (same seed), TEAL uniform. All seeds show max $\Delta$$\leq$1.70pp at 30\%; max $\Delta$$\leq$0.79pp at 50\% (s42). $^\dagger$s42 oracle is from an earlier training run; s123/s456 use matched retrained oracles.}
93	\label{tab:c1}
94	\resizebox{\columnwidth}{!}{
95	\begin{tabular}{lllccccc}
96	\toprule
97	Sparsity & Seed & Method & PPL$\downarrow$ & ARC-c & WG & MMLU & GSM8K \\
98	\midrule
99	\multirow{6}{*}{30\%}
100	& s42$^\dagger$ & C1 & 9.45 & 40.27 & 67.40 & \textbf{42.30} & \textbf{7.58} \\ % exp_id: c1_ablation_30pct_s42_eval_v2
101	& & KL & \textbf{8.80} & \textbf{40.78} & \textbf{68.59} & 40.6 & 7.35 \\ % exp_id: bench_kl30_uniform
102	& s123 & C1 & 8.84 & 44.71 & \textbf{69.77} & \textbf{39.92} & 6.82 \\ % exp_id: eval_c1_retrain_30pct_s123
103	& & KL & \textbf{8.83} & \textbf{45.31} & 69.38 & 39.4 & \textbf{7.51} \\ % exp_id: kl30_s123_eval
104	& s456 & C1 & 8.82 & 44.71 & \textbf{69.93} & 41.04 & 6.60 \\ % exp_id: eval_c1_retrain_30pct_s456
105	& & KL & \textbf{8.79} & \textbf{45.05} & 68.75 & \textbf{42.0} & \textbf{7.58} \\ % exp_id: kl30_s456_uniform_eval
106	\midrule
107	\multirow{2}{*}{50\%}
108	& s42 & C1 & 11.96 & 32.17 & \textbf{63.14} & 27.79 & \textbf{2.05} \\ % exp_id: c1_bce_kl_targets_teal50u
109	& & KL & 11.96 & \textbf{32.59} & 62.35 & \textbf{27.92} & 1.97 \\ % exp_id: c1_kl_teal50u
110	\bottomrule
111	\end{tabular}}
112	\end{table}
113	
114	At 50\%, C1 matches KL within statistical noise (max $\Delta$$\leq$0.79pp, s42), while magnitude-trained BCE yields PPL 28.13$\pm$0.20 ($2.4\times$ worse; Table~\ref{tab:main}).
115	At 30\%, all three C1 predictors reproduce KL quality within $\pm$1.70pp across downstream tasks (max $\Delta$=1.70pp MMLU, s42); retrained seeds (s123, s456) show tighter agreement ($\leq$1.18pp).
116	The seed-42 oracle was extracted from an earlier checkpoint with a minor PPL gap (0.65pp vs.\ $\leq$0.03pp for retrained seeds), but all three seeds confirm the core finding: mask quality, not gradient coupling, drives inference-stage perplexity.
117	These results confirm that KL's coupled gradients discover mask targets that yield lower perplexity and transfer to a simpler objective (BCE): mask quality is the proximate driver of inference-stage quality, while gradient coupling remains necessary at the discovery stage. % exp_id: bce50_3seed, c1_bce_kl_targets_30u, c1_s123
118	PPL transfers within 0.79pp; downstream metrics exhibit larger seed-dependent variation (up to 4.5pp on MMLU), suggesting that mask-quality signals captured by PPL do not fully predict task-specific performance.
119	
120	\paragraph{Soft-vs-Hard Confound.}
121	C1 changes both gradient coupling and mask representation (hard binary vs.\ soft Gumbel-Sigmoid). However, BCE with Gumbel-Sigmoid relaxation (even without penalty) yields PPL 32.22 (Table~\ref{tab:ablation}), worse than standard hard-target BCE (27.97): softness alone does not explain KL's advantage. % exp_id: bce_gumbel_no_penalty_s42, eval_bce_v3_s42
122	Disentangling target quality from gradient representation remains for future work.
123	
124	\subsection{Ablation Studies}
125	
126	Table~\ref{tab:ablation} isolates the contribution of each design choice at 50\% sparsity.
127	
128	\begin{table}[t]
129	\centering
130	\caption{Ablation at 50\% sparsity on LLaMA-3.1-8B (WikiText-2 PPL$\downarrow$, unified benchmark protocol).}
131	\label{tab:ablation}
132	\small
133	\begin{tabular}{lcc}
134	\toprule
135	Configuration & PPL$\downarrow$ & $\Delta$ from Ours \\
136	\midrule
137	\multicolumn{3}{l}{\textit{KL-based variants}} \\
138	Forward KL + Gumbel (Ours) & \textbf{11.79} & --- \\ % exp_id: bench_kl50_global
139	JSD (symmetric) + Gumbel & 12.54 & +0.75 \\ % exp_id: jsd_ablation_s42
140	Reverse KL + Gumbel & 12.80 & +1.01 \\ % exp_id: reverse_kl_ablation_s42
141	Forward KL + STE & 15.77 & +3.98 \\ % exp_id: ste_baseline_s42
142	\midrule
143	\multicolumn{3}{l}{\textit{Per-layer coupling (intermediate)}} \\
144	Per-layer MSE & 229.26 & +217.47 \\ % exp_id: mse50_uniform_s42
145	\midrule
146	\multicolumn{3}{l}{\textit{BCE-based variants}} \\
147	BCE (no compensation) & 27.97 & +16.18 \\ % exp_id: eval_bce_v3_s42
148	BCE + Gumbel (no penalty) & 32.22 & +20.43 \\ % exp_id: bce_gumbel_no_penalty_s42
149	BCE + Gumbel + Penalty & 38.02 & +26.23 \\ % exp_id: bce_gumbel_lagrangian_isolation
150	\bottomrule
151	\end{tabular}
152	\end{table}
153	
154	\paragraph{Gumbel vs.\ STE.}
155	Replacing Gumbel-Sigmoid with STE~\cite{bengio2013ste} degrades PPL from 11.79 to 15.77 (+3.98). % exp_id: ste_baseline_s42
156	
157	\paragraph{Divergence Direction.}
158	Forward KL outperforms JSD (+6.4\%) and reverse KL (+8.6\%), consistent with the SNR analysis (\S\ref{sec:method:gradient}); mode-covering ensures no important neuron is omitted, contrasting with reverse-KL generation~\cite{gu2024minillm}. % exp_id: jsd_ablation_s42, reverse_kl_ablation_s42
159	
160	\paragraph{SPON Contribution.}
161	Adding SPON~\cite{xu2026spon} bias correction reduces PPL from 12.08 to 11.79 (2.4\% relative; Appendix~\ref{app:spon}). % exp_id: spon_2048_s42, mvp_kl_norm_v2_s42
162	
163	\paragraph{Loss Function Isolation.}
164	Removing the penalty from BCE+Gumbel improves PPL (32.22 vs.\ 38.02), partly due to a sparsity gap (38.3\% training vs.\ 50\% target), but remains $2.7\times$ worse than KL (11.79) and worse than vanilla BCE at matched 50\% sparsity (27.97): Gumbel relaxation does not benefit BCE regardless of sparsity control, and KL's advantage stems from the loss function itself. % exp_id: bce_gumbel_no_penalty_s42, bce_gumbel_lagrangian_isolation, eval_bce_v3_s42
165	The loss effect ($\Delta$PPL=20.43) is $5.1\times$ the relaxation effect ($\Delta$PPL=3.98).
166	
167	\paragraph{Compensation Redundancy.}
168	Adding compensation~\cite{gautam2026fastforward} (+67.2M params) to KL provides no benefit at either sparsity level (PPL 8.97 vs.\ 8.96 at 30\%, identical at 50\%; Appendix~\ref{app:comp_convergence}). Compensation networks are designed for fixed-mask inference (DejaVu/FastForward) and do not transfer to TEAL's dynamic global allocation (TEAL eval PPL 44.54 vs.\ per-token PPL 25.44 for BCE+comp). % exp_id: kl_comp_30_s42, kl_comp_30_s456, kl_comp_50_s42, bce_comp_staged_s42
169	
170	\paragraph{Hyperparameter Robustness.}
171	Appendices~\ref{app:db_sensitivity}--\ref{app:training_convergence} confirm robustness to predictor capacity ($d_b$, $<$3\%; Appendix~\ref{app:db_sensitivity}), temperature schedule ($<$1.8\%; Appendix~\ref{app:tau_sensitivity}), and training steps (saturated by 800; Appendix~\ref{app:training_convergence}). % exp_id: db_sensitivity_sweep, tau_schedule_sweep, training_steps_sweep
172	
173	
174	\subsection{Sparsity Scaling and Regime Analysis}
175	\label{sec:scaling}
176	
177	Figure~\ref{fig:sparsity_sweep} shows PPL as a function of sparsity for the KL predictor under global allocation.
178	
179	\begin{figure}[t]
180	\centering
181	\includegraphics[width=\columnwidth]{fig2_sparsity_sweep.pdf}
182	\caption{Perplexity scaling with sparsity. The KL predictor's advantage grows disproportionately as more neurons are pruned.}
183	\label{fig:sparsity_sweep}
184	\end{figure}
185	
186	The regime curve (Figure~\ref{fig:sparsity_sweep}) is strictly monotonic from PPL 8.17 at 25\% to 11.79 at 50\% (global, seed 42), with KL/Dense degradation ranging from $1.34\times$ to $1.93\times$.
187	At matched points, KL improves over TEAL by 19\% at 30\% and 50\% at 50\%; at 70\%, where TEAL collapses (PPL 100.11), the advantage reaches 82\%. % exp_id: regime_kl25_global, regime_kl50_global, kl_70_global_teal
188	
189	\paragraph{Allocation Behavior Across Sparsity Levels.}
190	Table~\ref{tab:phase} compares global vs.\ uniform allocation at representative sparsity levels, revealing qualitative shifts in preference.
191	
192	\begin{table}[t]
193	\centering
194	\caption{Allocation analysis at key sparsity levels (LLaMA-3.1-8B, PPL and MMLU; full 6-level data with all downstream metrics in Appendix~\ref{app:alloc_full}). $\pm$: 3-seed; others: seed 42. $^\ast$High seed variance (25.3--43.5\%).}
195	\label{tab:phase}
196	\footnotesize
197	\begin{tabular}{llcc}
198	\toprule
199	Sparsity & Allocation & PPL$\downarrow$ & MMLU \\
200	\midrule
201	\multirow{2}{*}{25\%}
202	& Global & \textbf{8.17} & \textbf{46.6} \\ % exp_id: regime_kl25_global
203	& Uniform & 8.23 & 44.0 \\ % exp_id: regime_kl25_uniform
204	\midrule
205	\multirow{2}{*}{30\%}
206	& Global & 8.96$\pm$0.02 & 35.7$\pm$9.5$^\ast$ \\ % exp_id: kl30_3seed_global
207	& Uniform & \textbf{8.81$\pm$0.02} & \textbf{40.7$\pm$1.3} \\ % exp_id: bench_kl30_uniform
208	\midrule
209	\multirow{2}{*}{35\%}
210	& Global & \textbf{9.31} & \textbf{40.4} \\ % exp_id: regime_kl35_global
211	& Uniform & 9.44 & 37.8 \\ % exp_id: regime_kl35_uniform
212	\midrule
213	\multirow{2}{*}{50\%}
214	& Global & \textbf{11.79} & \textbf{28.1$\pm$0.2} \\ % exp_id: multi_seed_kl50_global
215	& Uniform & 11.92$\pm$0.07 & 28.0$\pm$0.6 \\ % exp_id: multi_seed_kl50_uniform
216	\bottomrule
217	\end{tabular}
218	\end{table}
219	
220	The analysis reveals \emph{non-monotonic} allocation preference with three regimes, complementing OWL's~\cite{yin2024owl} finding that non-uniform layerwise budgets improve pruning quality (full 6-level data in Appendix~\ref{app:alloc_full}):
221	
222	\noindent\textbf{Budget-surplus ($\leq$30\%).}
223	At 25\%, PPL differs by only 0.06. At 30\%, uniform achieves PPL 8.81$\pm$0.02 (3-seed) with $7.3\times$ smaller cross-seed MMLU $\sigma$ (1.3pp vs.\ 9.5pp). % exp_id: regime_kl25_global, regime_kl25_uniform, kl30_3seed
224	
225	\noindent\textbf{Budget-constrained (35--40\%).}
226	Global becomes preferable: at 35\%, MMLU 40.4\% vs.\ 37.8\% (+2.6pp); at 40\%, MMLU 38.1\% (Appendix~\ref{app:alloc_full}). % exp_id: regime_kl35_global, regime_kl35_uniform, regime_kl40_global
227	
228	\noindent\textbf{Capacity-limited ($\geq$45\%).}
229	Gap vanishes for reasoning: at 50\%, MMLU collapses to near-random under both strategies (28.1 vs.\ 28.0\%), though global still helps non-reasoning tasks (HellaSwag +2.29pp; Appendix~\ref{app:downstream}). Late-layer starvation persists (Figure~\ref{fig:retention}; Appendix~\ref{app:retention}). % exp_id: multi_seed_kl50_global, multi_seed_kl50_uniform, hellaswag_piqa_kl50_global, hellaswag_piqa_kl50_uniform
230	
231	
232	\paragraph{Cross-Architecture and Scale.}
233	\label{sec:cross_arch}
234	On Mistral-7B-v0.3~\cite{jiang2023mistral}, KL outperforms TEAL on 5/6 downstream
235	tasks at 30\% (HellaSwag +8.8pp, ARC +9.0pp, WG +5.6pp, PIQA +3.1pp, MMLU +0.5pp;
236	PPL 6.77 vs.\ 8.38); GSM8K is the sole reversal (11.1\% vs.\ 15.9\%,
237	Table~\ref{tab:mistral_downstream}); the 50\% capacity cliff also holds (Appendix~\ref{app:cross_arch}). % exp_id: mistral_kl30_downstream, mistral_teal30_downstream, mistral_teal50_downstream
238	On Qwen-2.5-14B~\cite{qwen2024qwen25}, KL's PPL advantage generalizes at 30\% ($1.52\times$ dense), but task-level effects diverge: WG improves (+3.0pp) while MMLU ($-$2.79pp, 3-seed) and GSM8K ($-$20pp; 32.1$\pm$5.2\% vs.\ 52.1\%, 3-seed) decline (Table~\ref{tab:14b}; actual sparsity: KL 29.6\%, TEAL 29.3\%); global allocation narrows MMLU to $-$8.1pp and equalizes GSM8K (\S\ref{sec:scaling}). % exp_id: qwen14b_kl30_uniform_4task, qwen14b_teal30_uniform
239	These effects are scale-dependent: at 8B all methods collapse to $<$9\% GSM8K; at 14B, TEAL retains 59\% of dense reasoning vs.\ KL's 37\% ($\sigma$=5.2pp).
240	Per-layer Jaccard variance is $2.4\times$ higher at 14B (std 0.076 vs.\ 0.031) with early-layer convergence (L3--L6) absent in 8B. % exp_id: qwen14b_kl30_uniform_4task, qwen14b_teal30_uniform % exp_id: qwen14b_kl30_s42, qwen14b_teal30_eval, qwen14b_bce30_s42_eval, qwen14b_kl30_uniform_4task, qwen14b_teal30_uniform_4task
241	\paragraph{Inference Efficiency.}
242	\label{sec:overhead}
243	The full sparse pipeline adds 6.4\% latency overhead (predictor: 2.7\%, mask application: 3.7\%; 75.96M params / 0.9\% of base; Appendix~\ref{app:latency}); net acceleration requires sparse kernel support. % exp_id: latency_profiling
244	
245	
```

KL/BCE predictors require multi-seed evaluation due to random initialization.}; (2) \\textbf{BCE predictor} (DejaVu-style~\\cite{liu2023dejavu}), trained with per-neuron binary cross-entropy on magnitude-based oracle masks for 1000 and 2700 steps; and (3) \\textbf{Uniform allocation}, which distributes the sparsity budget equally across layers.\n\n\n\\subsection{Main Results}\n\nTable~\\ref{tab:main} presents the primary comparison. Uniform allocation preserves downstream performance best; global allocation is analyzed to understand allocation-quality tradeoffs (\\S\\ref{sec:scaling}).\nAt 30\\% sparsity, KL distillation under uniform allocation achieves PPL 8.81$\\pm$0.02 (3-seed), reducing TEAL's perplexity by 19\\% (10.82 $\\to$ 8.81). % exp_id: bench_kl30_uniform, teal30_uniform\nHellaSwag (86.3\\% retention) and PIQA (92.6\\% retention) confirm moderate degradation on non-reasoning tasks (Appendix~\\ref{app:downstream}). % exp_id: hellaswag_piqa_kl30_uniform\nUniform allocation at 30\\% achieves PPL 8.81$\\pm$0.02 with stable downstream performance (MMLU 40.7$\\pm$1.3\\%, 3-seed; Method & PPL$\\downarrow$ & ARC-c & WG & MMLU & GSM8K \\\\\n\\midrule\n0\\% & Dense & 6.12 & 58.28 & 78.06 & 65.27 & 48.75 \\\\\n\\midrule\n\\multirow{5}{*}{30\\%}\n& TEAL uniform & 10.82 & 39.33 & kl30_s456_uniform\n\\midrule\n\\multirow{5}{*}{50\\%}\n& TEAL uniform & 23.90 & 27.47 & 54.38 & \\textbf{29.26} & 1.74 \\\\ % exp_id: teal50_uniform\n& BCE (2700 steps)$^\\ast$ & 28.13$\\pm$0.20 & 27.6$\\pm$0.6 & 51.8$\\pm$0.6 & 26.1$\\pm$0.5 & 2.1$\\pm$0.2 \\\\ % exp_id: bce50_3seed\n& MSE uniform & 229.26 & --- & --- & --- & --- \\\\ % exp_id: mse50_uniform_s42\n& KL global (Ours) & \\textbf{11.79} & 32.85 & \\textbf{63.46} & 28.1 & 1.29 \\\\ % exp_id: bench_kl50_global\n& KL uniform & 11.92$\\pm$0.07 & \\textbf{33.8$\\pm$1.2} & 62.9$\\pm$0.1 & 28.0$\\pm$0.6 & \\textbf{2.2$\\pm$0.1} \\\\ % exp_id: bench_kl50_uniform, bench_kl50_uniform_s123, bench_kl50_uniform_s456\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}}\n\\end{table}\n\n\n\\subsection{Extended Training Analysis}\n\\label{sec:isocompute}\n\nTo test whether BCE simply requires more compute, we train it for 2700 steps (2.71$\\times$ the KL step budget, approximately equal wall-clock time: $\\sim$46 min BCE vs.\\ $\\sim$45 min KL on RTX PRO 6000).\nThe BCE predictor achieves PPL 28.13$\\pm$0.20 (3-seed), still 2.39$\\times$ worse than KL at 1000 steps (PPL 11.79). % exp_id: bce50_3seed\nThe difference is systematic, not due to architectural or compute limitations: both predictors use identical architectures and TEAL allocation, yet BCE achieves +7pp oracle F1 (0.613 vs.\\ 0.540) with 139\\% worse PPL (28.13 vs.\\ 11.79). KL's near-random precision (0.51) is consistent with a mask optimized for distributional fidelity rather than magnitude agreement. % exp_id: f1_oracle_analysis, bce_isocompute_s42\nCoupled training correlates with better output preservation; the target-swap ablation in \\S\\ref{sec:mask_analysis} confirms that the discovered mask targets, not the coupling mechanism itself, drive inference-stage quality.\n\n\n\\subsection{Mask Pattern Analysis}\n\\label{sec:mask_analysis}\n\nTo understand \\emph{what} the KL predictor learns, we compare KL-derived masks against magnitude-based oracle masks (the BCE training targets) at 50\\% sparsity on WikiText-2.\nPer-layer Jaccard similarity averages 32\\% at 50\\% sparsity (64 samples, 131K tokens), computed per-token (each token's KL and magnitude masks are constructed independently, then Jaccard distances are averaged across tokens): the KL predictor discovers neuron importance patterns that diverge 68\\% from activation magnitude, with divergence scaling from 46\\% at 30\\% to 68\\% at 50\\% (Figure~\\ref{fig:mask_heatmap}).\nAt layer 16, 41\\% of neurons fall in disagreement quadrants where KL and magnitude rankings diverge (Appendix~\\ref{app:mask_viz}, Figures~\\ref{fig:mask_heatmap}--\\ref{fig:scatter}).\nDespite this divergence, KL achieves PPL 11.79 vs.\\ BCE's 28.13$\\pm$0.20: output-distribution fidelity, not magnitude agreement, produces lower-perplexity masks under global allocation. % exp_id: bench_kl50_global, bce50_3seed, c1_mask_overlap\n\n\\paragraph{Per-Layer Divergence Structure.}\nPer-layer Jaccard distance follows a U-shaped pattern (Figure~\\ref{fig:mask_heatmap}): at 50\\%, middle layers (L14--L17) show lowest divergence ($\\sim$0.61), while late layers (L22--L28) peak at $\\sim$0.70--0.75. The same structure holds at 30\\% (range 0.361--0.600).\nLate layers are closest to the KL-optimized output distribution, so their backward gradient carries stronger output-sensitivity signal and the predictor deviates most from magnitude ranking there. % exp_id: c1_mask_overlap\n\n\\paragraph{Target-Swap Ablation: Target Quality vs.\\ Gradient Coupling.}\nTo disentangle whether KL's advantage stems from its coupled gradient pathway or from the mask patterns it discovers, we train a standard BCE predictor on KL-derived oracle masks instead of magnitude-based masks (C1 target-swap ablation).\nTable~\\ref{tab:c1} reports results under TEAL uniform allocation at matched sparsity.\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\caption{Target-swap ablation (C1): BCE on KL-derived masks vs.\\ KL predictor (same seed), TEAL uniform. All seeds show max $\\Delta$$\\leq$1.70pp at 30\\%; max $\\Delta$$\\leq$0.79pp at 50\\% (s42). $^\\dagger$s42 oracle is from an earlier training run; s123/s456 use matched retrained oracles.}\n\\label{tab:c1}\n\\resizebox{\\columnwidth}{!}{\n\\begin{tabular}{lllccccc}\n\\toprule\nSparsity & Seed & Method & PPL$\\downarrow$ & ARC-c & WG & MMLU & GSM8K \\\\\n\\midrule\n\\multirow{6}{*}{30\\%}\n& s42$^\\dagger$ & C1 & 9.45 & 40.27 & 67.40 & \\textbf{42.30} & \\textbf{7.58} \\\\ % exp_id: c1_ablation_30pct_s42_eval_v2\n& & KL & \\textbf{8.80} & \\textbf{40.78} & \\textbf{68.59} & 40.6 & 7.35 \\\\ % exp_id: bench_kl30_uniform\n& s123 & C1 & 8.84 & 44.71 & \\textbf{69.77} & \\textbf{39.92} & 6.82 \\\\ % exp_id: eval_c1_retrain_30pct_s123\n& & KL & \\textbf{8.83} & \\textbf{45.31} & 69.38 & 39.4 & \\textbf{7.51} \\\\ % exp_id: kl30_s123_eval\n& s456 & C1 & 8.82 & 44.71 & \\textbf{69.93} & 41.04 & 6.60 \\\\ % exp_id: eval_c1_retrain_30pct_s456\n& & KL & \\textbf{8.79} & \\textbf{45.05} & 68.75 & \\textbf{42.0} & \\textbf{7.58} \\\\ % exp_id: kl30_s456_uniform_eval\n\\midrule\n\\multirow{2}{*}{50\\%}\n& s42 & C1 & 11.96 & 32.17 & \\textbf{63.14} & 27.79 & \\textbf{2.05} \\\\ % exp_id: c1_bce_kl_targets_teal50u\n& & KL & 11.96 & \\textbf{32.59} & 62.35 & \\textbf{27.92} & 1.97 \\\\ % exp_id: c1_kl_teal50u\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}}\n\\end{table}\n\nAt 50\\%, C1 matches KL within statistical noise (max $\\Delta$$\\leq$0.79pp, s42), while magnitude-trained BCE yields PPL 28.13$\\pm$0.20 ($2.4\\times$ worse; Table~\\ref{tab:main}).\nAt 30\\%, all three C1 predictors reproduce KL quality within $\\pm$1.70pp across downstream tasks (max $\\Delta$=1.70pp MMLU, s42); retrained seeds (s123, s456) show tighter agreement ($\\leq$1.18pp).\nThe seed-42 oracle was extracted from an earlier checkpoint with a minor PPL gap (0.65pp vs.\\ $\\leq$0.03pp for retrained seeds), but all three seeds confirm the core finding: mask quality, not gradient coupling, drives inference-stage perplexity.\nThese results confirm that KL's coupled gradients discover mask targets that yield lower perplexity and transfer to a simpler objective (BCE): mask quality is the proximate driver of inference-stage quality, while gradient coupling remains necessary at the discovery stage. % exp_id: bce50_3seed, c1_bce_kl_targets_30u, c1_s123\nPPL transfers within 0.79pp; downstream metrics exhibit larger seed-dependent variation (up to 4.5pp on MMLU), suggesting that mask-quality signals captured by PPL do not fully predict task-specific performance.\n\n\\paragraph{Soft-vs-Hard Confound.}\nC1 changes both gradient coupling and mask representation (hard binary vs.\\ soft Gumbel-Sigmoid). However, BCE with Gumbel-Sigmoid relaxation (even without penalty) yields PPL 32.22 (Table~\\ref{tab:ablation}), worse than standard hard-target BCE (27.97): softness alone does not explain KL's advantage. % exp_id: bce_gumbel_no_penalty_s42, eval_bce_v3_s42\nDisentangling target quality from gradient representation remains for future work.\n\n\\subsection{Ablation Studies}\n\nTable~\\ref{tab:ablation} isolates the contribution of each design choice at 50\\% sparsity.\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\caption{Ablation at 50\\% sparsity on LLaMA-3.1-8B (WikiText-2 PPL$\\downarrow$, unified benchmark protocol).}\n\\label{tab:ablation}\n\\small\n\\begin{tabular}{lcc}\n\\toprule\nConfiguration & PPL$\\downarrow$ & $\\Delta$ from Ours \\\\\n\\midrule\n\\multicolumn{3}{l}{\\textit{KL-based variants}} \\\\\nForward KL + Gumbel (Ours) & \\textbf{11.79} & --- \\\\ % exp_id: bench_kl50_global\nJSD (symmetric) + Gumbel & 12.54 & +0.75 \\\\ % exp_id: jsd_ablation_s42\nReverse KL + Gumbel & 12.80 & +1.01 \\\\ % exp_id: reverse_kl_ablation_s42\nForward KL + STE & 15.77 & +3.98 \\\\ % exp_id: ste_baseline_s42\n\\midrule\n\\multicolumn{3}{l}{\\textit{Per-layer coupling (intermediate)}} \\\\\nPer-layer MSE & 229.26 & +217.47 \\\\ % exp_id: mse50_uniform_s42\n\\midrule\n\\multicolumn{3}{l}{\\textit{BCE-based variants}} \\\\\nBCE (no compensation) & 27.97 & +16.18 \\\\ % exp_id: eval_bce_v3_s42\nBCE + Gumbel (no penalty) & 32.22 & +20.43 \\\\ % exp_id: bce_gumbel_no_penalty_s42\nBCE + Gumbel + Penalty & 38.02 & +26.23 \\\\ % exp_id: bce_gumbel_lagrangian_isolation\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\n\\paragraph{Gumbel vs.\\ STE.}\nReplacing Gumbel-Sigmoid with STE~\\cite{bengio2013ste} degrades PPL from 11.79 to 15.77 (+3.98). % exp_id: ste_baseline_s42\n\n\\paragraph{Divergence Direction.}\nForward KL outperforms JSD (+6.4\\%) and reverse KL (+8.6\\%), consistent with the SNR analysis (\\S\\ref{sec:method:gradient}); mode-covering ensures no important neuron is omitted, contrasting with reverse-KL generation~\\cite{gu2024minillm}. % exp_id: jsd_ablation_s42, reverse_kl_ablation_s42\n\n\\paragraph{SPON Contribution.}\nAdding SPON~\\cite{xu2026spon} bias correction reduces PPL from 12.08 to 11.79 (2.4\\% relative; Appendix~\\ref{app:spon}). % exp_id: spon_2048_s42, mvp_kl_norm_v2_s42\n\n\\paragraph{Loss Function Isolation.}\nRemoving the penalty from BCE+Gumbel improves PPL (32.22 vs.\\ 38.02), partly due to a sparsity gap (38.3\\% training vs.\\ 50\\% target), but remains $2.7\\times$ worse than KL (11.79) and worse than vanilla BCE at matched 50\\% sparsity (27.97): Gumbel relaxation does not benefit BCE regardless of sparsity control, and KL's advantage stems from the loss function itself. % exp_id: bce_gumbel_no_penalty_s42, bce_gumbel_lagrangian_isolation, eval_bce_v3_s42\nThe loss effect ($\\Delta$PPL=20.43) is $5.1\\times$ the relaxation effect ($\\Delta$PPL=3.98).\n\n\\paragraph{Compensation Redundancy.}\nAdding compensation~\\cite{gautam2026fastforward} (+67.2M params) to KL provides no benefit at either sparsity level (PPL 8.97 vs.\\ 8.96 at 30\\%, identical at 50\\%; Appendix~\\ref{app:comp_convergence}). Compensation networks are designed for fixed-mask inference (DejaVu/FastForward) and do not transfer to TEAL's dynamic global allocation (TEAL eval PPL 44.54 vs.\\ per-token PPL 25.44 for BCE+comp). % exp_id: kl_comp_30_s42, kl_comp_30_s456, kl_comp_50_s42, bce_comp_staged_s42\n\n\\paragraph{Hyperparameter Robustness.}\nAppendices~\\ref{app:db_sensitivity}--\\ref{app:training_convergence} confirm robustness to predictor capacity ($d_b$, $<$3\\%; Appendix~\\ref{app:db_sensitivity}), temperature schedule ($<$1.8\\%; Appendix~\\ref{app:tau_sensitivity}), and training steps (saturated by 800; Appendix~\\ref{app:training_convergence}). % exp_id: db_sensitivity_sweep, tau_schedule_sweep, training_steps_sweep\n\n\n\\subsection{Sparsity Scaling and Regime Analysis}\n\\label{sec:scaling}\n\nFigure~\\ref{fig:sparsity_sweep} shows PPL as a function of sparsity for the KL predictor under global allocation.\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{fig2_sparsity_sweep.pdf}\n\\caption{Perplexity scaling with sparsity. The KL predictor's advantage grows disproportionately as more neurons are pruned.}\n\\label{fig:sparsity_sweep}\n\\end{figure}\n\nThe regime curve (Figure~\\ref{fig:sparsity_sweep}) is strictly monotonic from PPL 8.17 at 25\\% to 11.79 at 50\\% (global, seed 42), with KL/Dense degradation ranging from $1.34\\times$ to $1.93\\times$.\nAt matched points, KL improves over TEAL by 19\\% at 30\\% and 50\\% at 50\\%; at 70\\%, where TEAL collapses (PPL 100.11), the advantage reaches 82\\%. % exp_id: regime_kl25_global, regime_kl50_global, kl_70_global_teal\n\n\\paragraph{Allocation Behavior Across Sparsity Levels.}\nTable~\\ref{tab:phase} compares global vs.\\ uniform allocation at representative sparsity levels, revealing qualitative shifts in preference.\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\caption{Allocation analysis at key sparsity levels (LLaMA-3.1-8B, PPL and MMLU; full 6-level data with all downstream metrics in Appendix~\\ref{app:alloc_full}). $\\pm$: 3-seed; others: seed 42. $^\\ast$High seed variance (25.3--43.5\\%).}\n\\label{tab:phase}\n\\footnotesize\n\\begin{tabular}{llcc}\n\\toprule\nSparsity & Allocation & PPL$\\downarrow$ & MMLU \\\\\n\\midrule\n\\multirow{2}{*}{25\\%}\n& Global & \\textbf{8.17} & \\textbf{46.6} \\\\ % exp_id: regime_kl25_global\n& Uniform & 8.23 & 44.0 \\\\ % exp_id: regime_kl25_uniform\n\\midrule\n\\multirow{2}{*}{30\\%}\n& Global & 8.96$\\pm$0.02 & 35.7$\\pm$9.5$^\\ast$ \\\\ % exp_id: kl30_3seed_global\n& Uniform & \\textbf{8.81$\\pm$0.02} & \\textbf{40.7$\\pm$1.3} \\\\ % exp_id: bench_kl30_uniform\n\\midrule\n\\multirow{2}{*}{35\\%}\n& Global & \\textbf{9.31} & \\textbf{40.4} \\\\ % exp_id: regime_kl35_global\n& Uniform & 9.44 & 37.8 \\\\ % exp_id: regime_kl35_uniform\n\\midrule\n\\multirow{2}{*}{50\\%}\n& Global & \\textbf{11.79} & \\textbf{28.1$\\pm$0.2} \\\\ % exp_id: multi_seed_kl50_global\n& Uniform & 11.92$\\pm$0.07 & 28.0$\\pm$0.6 \\\\ % exp_id: multi_seed_kl50_uniform\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\nThe analysis reveals \\emph{non-monotonic} allocation preference with three regimes, complementing OWL's~\\cite{yin2024owl} finding that non-uniform layerwise budgets improve pruning quality (full 6-level data in Appendix~\\ref{app:alloc_full}):\n\n\\noindent\\textbf{Budget-surplus ($\\leq$30\\%).}\nAt 25\\%, PPL differs by only 0.06. At 30\\%, uniform achieves PPL 8.81$\\pm$0.02 (3-seed) with $7.3\\times$ smaller cross-seed MMLU $\\sigma$ (1.3pp vs.\\ 9.5pp). % exp_id: regime_kl25_global, regime_kl25_uniform, kl30_3seed\n\n\\noindent\\textbf{Budget-constrained (35--40\\%).}\nGlobal becomes preferable: at 35\\%, MMLU 40.4\\% vs.\\ 37.8\\% (+2.6pp); at 40\\%, MMLU 38.1\\% (Appendix~\\ref{app:alloc_full}). % exp_id: regime_kl35_global, regime_kl35_uniform, regime_kl40_global\n\n\\noindent\\textbf{Capacity-limited ($\\geq$45\\%).}\nGap vanishes for reasoning: at 50\\%, MMLU collapses to near-random under both strategies (28.1 vs.\\ 28.0\\%), though global still helps non-reasoning tasks (HellaSwag +2.29pp; Appendix~\\ref{app:downstream}). Late-layer starvation persists (Figure~\\ref{fig:retention}; Appendix~\\ref{app:retention}). % exp_id: multi_seed_kl50_global, multi_seed_kl50_uniform, hellaswag_piqa_kl50_global, hellaswag_piqa_kl50_uniform\n\n\n\\paragraph{Cross-Architecture and Scale.}\n\\label{sec:cross_arch}\nOn Mistral-7B-v0.3~\\cite{jiang2023mistral}, KL outperforms TEAL on 5/6 downstream\ntasks at 30\\% (HellaSwag +8.8pp, ARC +9.0pp, WG +5.6pp, PIQA +3.1pp, MMLU +0.5pp;\nPPL 6.77 vs.\\ 8.38); GSM8K is the sole reversal (11.1\\% vs.\\ 15.9\\%,\nTable~\\ref{tab:mistral_downstream}); the 50\\% capacity cliff also holds (Appendix~\\ref{app:cross_arch}). % exp_id: mistral_kl30_downstream, mistral_teal30_downstream, mistral_teal50_downstream\nOn Qwen-2.5-14B~\\cite{qwen2024qwen25}, KL's PPL advantage generalizes at 30\\% ($1.52\\times$ dense), but task-level effects diverge: WG improves (+3.0pp) while MMLU ($-$2.79pp, 3-seed) and GSM8K ($-$20pp; 32.1$\\pm$5.2\\% vs.\\ 52.1\\%, 3-seed) decline (Table~\\ref{tab:14b}; actual sparsity: KL 29.6\\%, TEAL 29.3\\%); global allocation narrows MMLU to $-$8.1pp and equalizes GSM8K (\\S\\ref{sec:scaling}). % exp_id: qwen14b_kl30_uniform_4task, qwen14b_teal30_uniform\nThese effects are scale-dependent: at 8B all methods collapse to $<$9\\% GSM8K; at 14B, TEAL retains 59\\% of dense reasoning vs.\\ KL's 37\\% ($\\sigma$=5.2pp).\nPer-layer Jaccard variance is $2.4\\times$ higher at 14B (std 0.076 vs.\\ 0.031) with early-layer convergence (L3--L6) absent in 8B. % exp_id: qwen14b_kl30_uniform_4task, qwen14b_teal30_uniform % exp_id: qwen14b_kl30_s42, qwen14b_teal30_eval, qwen14b_bce30_s42_eval, qwen14b_kl30_uniform_4task, qwen14b_teal30_uniform_4task\n\\paragraph{Inference Efficiency.}\n\\label{sec:overhead}\nThe full sparse pipeline adds 6.4\\% latency overhead (predictor: 2.7\\%, mask application: 3.7\\%; 75.96M params / 0.9\\% of base; Appendix~\\ref{app:latency}); net acceleration requires sparse kernel support. % exp_id: latency_profiling\n\n",

1	\section{Conclusion}
2	\label{sec:conclusion}
3	\vspace{-18pt}
4	
5	A target-swap ablation confirms that KL-discovered mask patterns (46--68\% divergent from magnitude) drive inference-stage quality.
6	A predictor ($<$1\% of base) reduces TEAL's perplexity by 19\% at 30\%, renders compensation redundant, and delivers $7.3\times$ smaller cross-seed $\sigma$ under uniform allocation.
7	Cross-architecture evaluation confirms the pattern (Mistral-7B: 5/6 tasks); at 14B, reasoning declines despite PPL gains.
8	Future work: MSE intermediate coupling, mask divergence analysis, and N:M sparsity.

{
  "replace_all": false,
  "file_path": "/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0509_14/projects/distill_sparse_swiglu/paper/experiments.tex",
  "old_string": "\\paragraph{Cross-Architecture and Scale.}\n\\label{sec:cross_arch}\nOn Mistral-7B-v0.3~\\cite{jiang2023mistral}, KL outperforms TEAL on 5/6 downstream\ntasks at 30\\% (HellaSwag +8.8pp, ARC +9.0pp, WG +5.6pp, PIQA +3.1pp, MMLU +0.5pp;\nPPL 6.77 vs.\\ 8.38); GSM8K is the sole reversal (11.1\\% vs.\\ 15.9\\%,\nTable~\\ref{tab:mistral_downstream}); the 50\\% capacity cliff also holds (Appendix~\\ref{app:cross_arch}). % exp_id: mistral_kl30_downstream, mistral_teal30_downstream, mistral_teal50_downstream\nOn Qwen-2.5-14B~\\cite{qwen2024qwen25}, KL's PPL advantage generalizes at 30\\% ($1.52\\times$ dense), but task-level effects diverge: WG improves (+3.0pp) while MMLU ($-$2.79pp, 3-seed) and GSM8K ($-$20pp; 32.1$\\pm$5.2\\% vs.\\ 52.1\\%, 3-seed) decline (Table~\\ref{tab:14b}; actual sparsity: KL 29.6\\%, TEAL 29.3\\%); global allocation narrows MMLU to $-$8.1pp and equalizes GSM8K (\\S\\ref{sec:scaling}). % exp_id: qwen14b_kl30_uniform_4task, qwen14b_teal30_uniform\nThese effects are scale-dependent: at 8B all methods collapse to $<$9\\% GSM8K; at 14B, TEAL retains 59\\% of dense reasoning vs.\\ KL's 37\\% ($\\sigma$=5.2pp).\nPer-layer Jaccard variance is $2.4\\times$ higher at 14B (std 0.076 vs.\\ 0.031) with early-layer convergence (L3--L6) absent in 8B. % exp_id: qwen14b_kl30_uniform_4task, qwen14b_teal30_uniform % exp_id: qwen14b_kl30_s42, qwen14b_teal30_eval, qwen14b_bce30_s42_eval, qwen14b_kl30_uniform_4task, qwen14b_teal30_uniform_4task\n\\paragraph{Inference Efficiency.}\n\\label{sec:overhead}\nThe full sparse pipeline adds 6.4\\% latency overhead (predictor: 2.7\\%, mask application: 3.7\\%; 75.96M params / 0.9\\% of base; Appendix~\\ref{app:latency}); net acceleration requires sparse kernel support. % exp_id: latency_profiling",
  "new_string": "\\paragraph{Cross-Architecture and Scale.}\n\\label{sec:cross_arch}\nOn Mistral-7B-v0.3~\\cite{jiang2023mistral}, KL outperforms TEAL on 5/6 downstream tasks at 30\\% (ARC +9.0pp, HellaSwag +8.8pp, WG +5.6pp; GSM8K reverses; Table~\\ref{tab:mistral_downstream}). % exp_id: mistral_kl30_downstream, mistral_teal30_downstream\nOn Qwen-2.5-14B~\\cite{qwen2024qwen25}, KL's PPL advantage generalizes ($1.52\\times$ dense) but task-level effects diverge: WG improves (+3.0pp) while MMLU ($-$2.79pp) and GSM8K ($-$20pp, 3-seed) decline (Table~\\ref{tab:14b}); these scale-dependent effects---at 8B all methods collapse to $<$9\\% GSM8K while at 14B TEAL retains 59\\% of dense reasoning vs.\\ KL's 37\\%---suggest that KL-discovered masks may interact differently with task-specific circuits at larger scales. % exp_id: qwen14b_kl30_uniform_4task, qwen14b_teal30_uniform\nThe full sparse pipeline adds 6.4\\% latency overhead (Appendix~\\ref{app:latency}); net acceleration requires sparse kernel support. % exp_id: latency_profiling"
}
